 | Section A: Listening (40 marks)
Instructions:
You will hear each recording twice.
Listen carefully and answer the questions.

For Q11ΓÇô20, write one word/number/date/time in the blank.

 | Transcript (Teacher/Audio):
The woman is wearing a striped purple dress with white shoes. She's holding a black umbrella while walking her brown poodle. Which picture shows her?
A boy is flying a yellow kite shaped like a diamond in the park. Two birds are flying higher than the kite. Which picture matches?
In the library, a librarian is stacking books while a girl in a pink headphone reads at the table. A boy nearby is using a laptop. Identify the scene.
At the beach, a man in blue shorts is building a sandcastle while a woman in a hat takes photos. A seagull is stealing their chips. Which picture shows this?
The chef is chopping vegetables while wearing a tall white hat. On the stove, a pot is boiling but no oven is being used.
Select the correct image.
In the garden, an old man waters sunflowers with a green can. A black cat watches from the fence behind him. Which picture matches?
At the zoo, a zookeeper feeds bananas to a sitting elephant. Two monkeys are swinging in the background. Identify the correct scene.
In the music class, a teacher plays guitar while two students clap. One student has drumsticks but isn't using them. Which picture shows this?
At the market, a vendor sells oranges and apples. A customer in a yellow sari examines mangoes while holding a woven basket. Select the correct image.
A postal worker rides a red bicycle past a bakery. A delivery truck is parked outside the pharmacy across the street. Which picture matches?
Student Task: For each question, circle A, B, C or D under the picture you hear. | Transcript (Teacher/Audio):

 | Part 2 (10 marks) | Part 2 (10 marks) | Part 2 (10 marks) | Part 2 (10 marks) | Part 2 (10 marks) | Part 2 (10 marks) | 
 | Transcript (Teacher/Audio):

The school canteen closes at 2:30 in the afternoon.
The new student's favorite colour is purple.
Their science project is due on March 15th.
A movie ticket for children costs 120 rupees.
The history exam will cover Chapter 7.
The football match starts at quarter past eleven.
The librarian's office is on the third floor.
They need two kilograms of flour for baking.
The post office is opposite the bank.
Her sister was born in August.

 | Young PhotographersΓÇÖ Journals
Three photography enthusiasts share their experiences:
Alex
I started documenting street art in my city last year. My blog features photos of murals and interviews with artists. I always note the exact locations and materials used (like spray paint vs. stencils) because followers want to visit these spots. My brother helps me edit videos showing the creation process. The most rewarding part is discovering hidden artworks through readersΓÇÖ tips.
Priya
My focus is wildlife photography. I post weekly from nature reserves, explaining how I capture animals without disturbing them. At first, I hesitated to share my work, but a teacher urged me to try. My ΓÇ£bird nestingΓÇ¥ series went viral! IΓÇÖve started adding short audio clips of animal sounds, which viewers love. This hobby combines my passion for nature and tech skills.
Omar
I use my blog as a visual diary of architectural wonders. Each post compares old and new buildings in my neighborhood. What began as a school project now has international readers who suggest historical facts about structures. When exams pile up, I post less frequently, but I always return ΓÇô this blog is my creative anchor. | Young PhotographersΓÇÖ Journals

 | The Green Earth Initiative
In 2024, a team of scientists and activists from Brazil, Japan and Kenya launched the Green Earth Initiative to combat deforestation. Dr. Amina Mwangi, a Kenyan ecologist, leads the project, which uses AI-powered drones to monitor rainforests in the Amazon and Congo Basin.
The initiative partners with Indigenous communities like the Yanomami tribe in Brazil to plant 10 million trees annually. In Japan, students from Tokyo University developed an app called EcoTrack to crowdsource deforestation alerts. "Global problems need global solutions," says Dr. Mwangi.
A breakthrough came in March 2025 when the team discovered a new carbon-absorbing moss species in Mount Kenya. This moss, named Bryum viridis, can store 20% more COΓéé than ordinary plants. The project now expands to IndonesiaΓÇÖs peatlands, funded by a UN grant. | The Green Earth Initiative

 | Questions 26ΓÇô30
For each question, choose the correct answer.
The Amazon Rainforest: EarthΓÇÖs Lungs
The Amazon Rainforest, spanning nine countries in South America, covers over 5.5 million square kilometers. It produces 20% of the worldΓÇÖs oxygen and houses 10% of all known species, including jaguars, pink river dolphins and 16,000 tree varieties. Indigenous tribes like the Yanomami have lived here sustainably for millennia.
Ecotourism has grown rapidly, with visitors hiking, birdwatching, or exploring the Amazon River. Biologists study its biodiversity, calling it a ΓÇ£living laboratoryΓÇ¥ for climate research.
Sadly, deforestation threatens the Amazon. Illegal logging, cattle ranching and wildfires destroy 20,000 square kilometers annually. This disrupts rainfall patterns and releases stored carbon, worsening global warming.
Conservation groups like WWF and local governments now enforce stricter laws. Reforestation projects and ΓÇ£carbon creditΓÇ¥ programs aim to balance economic needs with ecological preservation. | Questions 26ΓÇô30
For each question, choose the correct answer.

 | Answer the given questions (31-35) in approx. 1-2 sentences. 
The Solar Power Revolution

Solar energy has emerged as a leading renewable power source, with global capacity doubling every 3ΓÇô4 years. Unlike fossil fuels, solar panels generate electricity without emissions, reducing reliance on coal and oil. Countries like Germany and China now derive over 10% of their energy from solar farms and rooftop installations.
Governments incentivise solar adoption through tax rebates and "net metering" policies, allowing households to sell excess energy back to grids. Innovations like perovskite solar cells promise higher efficiency at lower costs, with companies like Tesla integrating solar roofs into mainstream housing.
Challenges persist. Solar farms require vast land areas, raising concerns about habitat disruption. Energy storage remains problematicΓÇöbatteries struggle to retain power during cloudy periods. In developing nations, upfront costs and lack of infrastructure hinder adoption despite abundant sunlight.
GermanyΓÇÖs Energiewende (energy transition) demonstrates success: 50% of its electricity now comes from renewables, including solar. Critics argue this transition relies heavily on subsidies, but proponents highlight long-term savings and job creation in green industries.
Experts predict solar could supply 30% of global energy by 2040 if storage technology improves. Combined with wind and hydropower, it offers a viable path to decarbonisation. | Answer the given questions (31-35) in approx. 1-2 sentences.

 | 31. Why might governments prioritise solar energy subsidies over direct fossil fuel regulation?
Governments likely subsidise solar energy to encourage voluntary adoption while avoiding political resistance from fossil fuel industries. This approach balances environmental goals with economic stability during the transition.
32. What does GermanyΓÇÖs Energiewende reveal about public attitudes toward renewable energy?
GermanyΓÇÖs success suggests public support for renewables grows when policies align with economic benefits (e.g., job creation) and energy independence, even if initial costs are high.
33. How could improved battery technology indirectly address habitat disruption from solar farms?
Better storage would reduce the need for large solar farms by maximising energy output from smaller installations (e.g., rooftops), minimising land-use conflicts with ecosystems.
34. Why might solar adoption in developing nations lag despite abundant sunlight?
High upfront costs, lack of financing options and unreliable grids create barriers, even when sunlight is plentiful. This highlights the need for international funding and localised infrastructure plans.
35. ΓÇ£Solar alone cannot decarbonise economies.ΓÇ¥ Do you agree? Justify with reasoning.
Agree. SolarΓÇÖs intermittency requires complementary technologies (wind, nuclear) and grid modernisation. Industrial sectors like steel production also need alternative solutions (e.g., hydrogen) to achieve full decarbonisation. | 31.  | Alan Turing: The Codebreaker Who Shaped the Future
Alan Turing, born in 1912 in England, was a mathematician and computer scientist. During World War II, he played a crucial role in breaking German codes, which 36________ the warΓÇÖs outcome. His work on the Enigma machine saved countless lives, but his contributions remained classified for decades.
Turing also laid the foundation for modern computing with his concept of the Turing Machine, a theoretical model that 37________ how computers process information. Despite his genius, he faced persecution for being gay, which was illegal in Britain at the time. In 1952, he was arrested and chemically castrated, yet he 38________ his research until his tragic death in 1954.
Today, Turing is celebrated as a pioneer of artificial intelligence. His legacy 39________ debates about ethics in technology and LGBTQ+ rights. Many argue that his persecution 40________ scientific progress for years, as his later ideas were never fully explored. | Alan Turing: The Codebreaker Who Shaped the Future

 | Rosalind Franklin: The Overlooked Pioneer of DNA
Rosalind Franklin, born in 1920 in London, was a chemist whose work 41________ the discovery of DNAΓÇÖs double-helix structure. Using X-ray crystallography, she captured Photo 51, a critical image that 42________ crucial clues about DNAΓÇÖs shape. However, her contributions were often overshadowed by her male colleagues at the time.
Despite her groundbreaking research, Franklin 43________ full recognition during her lifetime. Her data was shared without her consent and the Nobel Prize for DNAΓÇÖs discovery was awarded to Watson, Crick and Wilkins in 1962, four years after her death. Today, historians and scientists 44________ her role as essential to one of biologyΓÇÖs greatest breakthroughs.
FranklinΓÇÖs story 45________ the importance of ethical collaboration and the need to acknowledge underrepresented voices in science. | Rosalind Franklin: The Overlooked Pioneer of DNA

FranklinΓÇÖs story 45________ the importance of ethical collaboration and the need to acknowledge underrepresented voices in science. | Rosalind Franklin: The Overlooked Pioneer of DNA
Rosalind Franklin, born in 1920 in London, was a chemist whose work 41________ the discovery of DNAΓÇÖs double-helix structure. Using X-ray crystallography, she captured Photo 51, a critical image that 42________ crucial clues about DNAΓÇÖs shape. However, her contributions were often overshadowed by her male colleagues at the time.
Despite her groundbreaking research, Franklin 43________ full recognition during her lifetime. Her data was shared without her consent and the Nobel Prize for DNAΓÇÖs discovery was awarded to Watson, Crick and Wilkins in 1962, four years after her death.
